I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.

It used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.

Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.

But it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.

So yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.

I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.

It’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.

I’m not really sure. I’ve certainly had sad periods in my life, you know, times when things felt harder or I was discouraged for a while, but I’m having trouble sorting out whether any of those were truly like this and lasted at least two weeks in the same way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.

But then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.

So… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. It feels connected to what happened with him, definitely, but whether it’s out of proportion, I honestly don’t know.

  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.9, F43.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well... I think what finally pushed me to come in is that I scared myself a little. I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.\n\nIt’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this period began, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve certainly had sad periods in my life, you know, times when things felt harder or I was discouraged for a while, but I’m having trouble sorting out whether any of those were truly like this and lasted at least two weeks in the same way.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m just remembering ordinary difficult times and stretching them into something more than they were. This feels particular enough that I’m not immediately thinking, “Oh yes, this happened before.” But I also don’t keep a perfect record of my moods, especially going back over many years. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. I’m still not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.

There were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.

So I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.

I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.

And once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.

By morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.
=====================================

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well... I think what finally pushed me to come in is that I scared myself a little. I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.\n\nIt’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this period began, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve certainly had sad periods in my life, you know, times when things felt harder or I was discouraged for a while, but I’m having trouble sorting out whether any of those were truly like this and lasted at least two weeks in the same way.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m just remembering ordinary difficult times and stretching them into something more than they were. This feels particular enough that I’m not immediately thinking, “Oh yes, this happened before.” But I also don’t keep a perfect record of my moods, especially going back over many years. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. It feels connected to what happened with him, definitely, but whether it’s out of proportion, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your life, was there ever a time before these last eight months when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.\n\nThere were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.\n\nAnd once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.\n\nBy morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can say that, no—not with any certainty. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I was sad, or worn down, or not myself for a while, like anyone does by this age. But when you ask whether it was this same kind of depression, nearly every day, for at least two weeks… I can’t really identify a time before these last eight months that clearly fits that.

I keep trying to search my memory, and some things blur together. There were hard times, certainly, but I don’t remember it being like this in the same steady, day-after-day way. So I suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not aware of a previous episode like this. If there was one, it isn’t something I can point to and describe confidently.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well... I think what finally pushed me to come in is that I scared myself a little. I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.\n\nIt’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this period began, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve certainly had sad periods in my life, you know, times when things felt harder or I was discouraged for a while, but I’m having trouble sorting out whether any of those were truly like this and lasted at least two weeks in the same way.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m just remembering ordinary difficult times and stretching them into something more than they were. This feels particular enough that I’m not immediately thinking, “Oh yes, this happened before.” But I also don’t keep a perfect record of my moods, especially going back over many years. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. It feels connected to what happened with him, definitely, but whether it’s out of proportion, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your life, was there ever a time before these last eight months when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.\n\nThere were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.\n\nAnd once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.\n\nBy morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.\n\nDoctor: Before these last eight months, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that, no—not with any certainty. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I was sad, or worn down, or not myself for a while, like anyone does by this age. But when you ask whether it was this same kind of depression, nearly every day, for at least two weeks… I can’t really identify a time before these last eight months that clearly fits that.\n\nI keep trying to search my memory, and some things blur together. There were hard times, certainly, but I don’t remember it being like this in the same steady, day-after-day way. So I suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not aware of a previous episode like this. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.\n\nThere were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.\n\nAnd once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.\n\nBy morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.\n\nDoctor: Before these last eight months, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that, no—not with any certainty. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I’m not really sure that I’ve noticed a clear change, to be honest. I mean, my eating probably varies a bit from day to day, like it always has, but I can’t say there’s been some obvious pattern where I’m much hungrier or not hungry at all.

Sometimes I’ll think, “Did I eat properly today?” but that may just be because I’m paying more attention now that you’re asking. I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major appetite change that stands out to me.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well... I think what finally pushed me to come in is that I scared myself a little. I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.\n\nIt’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this period began, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no.   {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most days I feel as if a gray film has settled over everything, and even when I’m making tea or looking out at my garden, there’s this quiet heaviness in me that I can’t quite reason my way out of.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, but I’ll sit there staring at the paper and put it all away because the pleasure I used to feel in color and light just doesn’t seem to arrive.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: At night I fall asleep unevenly and then wake around three or four thinking about phone calls, forms, and whether I handled things correctly, and by morning I feel as if I’ve hardly rested.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; There were hard times, certainly, but I don’t remember it being like this in the same steady, day-after-day way. So I suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not aware of a previous episode like this. If there was one, it isn’t something I can point to and describe confidently.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I’ve noticed a clear change, to be honest. I mean, my eating probably varies a bit from day to day, like it always has, but I can’t say there’s been some obvious pattern where I’m much hungrier or not hungry at all.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Did I eat properly today?” but that may just be because I’m paying more attention now that you’re asking. I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major appetite change that stands out to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this period, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always use that exact word in my head. It’s more that I keep coming back to my brother and how he declined, and I go over it again and again, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself.

I’ll think, if I had been a better sister, if I’d been paying closer attention, if I had called more, asked different questions, noticed something sooner… maybe I could have prevented some of what happened, or at least kept it from getting as bad as it did. And I know, part of me knows, that I may be expecting too much of myself looking back on it now, but it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s like I should have known. I should have seen it.

So yes, I’ve felt… not worthless exactly every minute, but guilty, inadequate, like I failed him in some important way. And that feeling has been very hard to set down.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well... I think what finally pushed me to come in is that I scared myself a little. I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.\n\nIt’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this period began, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve certainly had sad periods in my life, you know, times when things felt harder or I was discouraged for a while, but I’m having trouble sorting out whether any of those were truly like this and lasted at least two weeks in the same way.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m just remembering ordinary difficult times and stretching them into something more than they were. This feels particular enough that I’m not immediately thinking, “Oh yes, this happened before.” But I also don’t keep a perfect record of my moods, especially going back over many years. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. It feels connected to what happened with him, definitely, but whether it’s out of proportion, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your life, was there ever a time before these last eight months when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.\n\nThere were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.\n\nAnd once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.\n\nBy morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.\n\nDoctor: Before these last eight months, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that, no—not with any certainty. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I was sad, or worn down, or not myself for a while, like anyone does by this age. But when you ask whether it was this same kind of depression, nearly every day, for at least two weeks… I can’t really identify a time before these last eight months that clearly fits that.\n\nI keep trying to search my memory, and some things blur together. If there was one, it isn’t something I can point to and describe confidently.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I’ve noticed a clear change, to be honest. I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. It feels connected to what happened with him, definitely, but whether it’s out of proportion, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your life, was there ever a time before these last eight months when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.\n\nThere were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.\n\nAnd once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.\n\nBy morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.\n\nDoctor: Before these last eight months, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that, no—not with any certainty. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I was sad, or worn down, or not myself for a while, like anyone does by this age. But when you ask whether it was this same kind of depression, nearly every day, for at least two weeks… I can’t really identify a time before these last eight months that clearly fits that.\n\nI keep trying to search my memory, and some things blur together. There were hard times, certainly, but I don’t remember it being like this in the same steady, day-after-day way. So I suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not aware of a previous episode like this. If there was one, it isn’t something I can point to and describe confidently.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I’ve noticed a clear change, to be honest. I mean, my eating probably varies a bit from day to day, like it always has, but I can’t say there’s been some obvious pattern where I’m much hungrier or not hungry at all.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Did I eat properly today?” but that may just be because I’m paying more attention now that you’re asking. I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major appetite change that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always use that exact word in my head. It’s more that I keep coming back to my brother and how he declined, and I go over it again and again, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself.\n\nI’ll think, if I had been a better sister, if I’d been paying closer attention, if I had called more, asked different questions, noticed something sooner… maybe I could have prevented some of what happened, or at least kept it from getting as bad as it did. And I know, part of me knows, that I may be expecting too much of myself looking back on it now, but it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s like I should have known. I should have seen it.\n\nSo yes, I’ve felt… not worthless exactly every minute, but guilty, inadequate, like I failed him in some important way. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well... I think what finally pushed me to come in is that I scared myself a little. I’ve been having these thoughts—not that I have a plan to do anything, I don’t—but thoughts like, “Maybe it would just be simpler if I didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And I know that’s not a good place for my mind to be going.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud, honestly. I kept telling myself it was just a passing thought, that people probably have dark thoughts when they’re overwhelmed or tired, but it’s been enough that I thought, no, I should probably talk to someone before I pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has this low mood been present most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels long, but it’s been more like… about eight months, I think. It really started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That’s when I noticed this heaviness settling in most days, and it just hasn’t really lifted the way I kept hoping it would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood is mainly a reaction to what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least that’s when it really began. It’s hard for me to separate it, because before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this particular low mood — the one that’s been hanging around and not really letting up — it started around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nIt’s been about eight months now. I kept thinking maybe I was just reacting to the shock of it, or adjusting, and that with time I’d feel more like myself again. But it’s gone on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or a temporary reaction anymore. Still, if you’re asking what it’s tied to in my mind, yes, I do connect it very strongly to what happened with him.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I notice it most with my watercolor painting. I still go through the motions sometimes — I’ll take out the paints, set up the little cup of water, tape the paper down, all of that — but then I just sit there looking at the blank page.\n\nIt used to feel soothing, almost absorbing, choosing colors and watching the light come through them. Now it’s like I’m waiting for that feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. After a while I’ll put everything away again, feeling sort of foolish for even trying. It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like painting anymore. It’s more like the pleasure part of it is missing.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart. I’m still managing the basics — I keep the house going, I get meals together, I pay the bills, that sort of thing. From the outside, someone might think I’m doing alright because the essentials are getting done.\n\nBut it’s changed the way I live my days. I used to go regularly with my walking group, and I’ve pulled back from that quite a bit. I’ll tell myself I’m going to go, I’ll even get my shoes by the door sometimes, and then I just… don’t follow through. And with mentoring, too — that used to mean a lot to me, being present for someone, listening carefully, encouraging them — but lately I don’t feel fully there with people. I find myself worrying that I’m not giving them what I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s making daily life harder, just maybe in a quieter way. I’m functioning, but I’m not really participating the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling excessively worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive worry, exactly. I mean, I do think about things — probably more than I used to — and I can get caught up in my head a bit, especially when I’m already feeling low. But when you say “anxious,” I’m not completely sure whether what I’m feeling fits that.\n\nIt’s more like a heaviness or a preoccupation than that keyed-up, panicky feeling I imagine when people talk about anxiety. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about lots of different things or feeling on edge all day. There are moments, certainly, but I haven’t really noticed anxiety as the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this period began, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve certainly had sad periods in my life, you know, times when things felt harder or I was discouraged for a while, but I’m having trouble sorting out whether any of those were truly like this and lasted at least two weeks in the same way.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if I’m just remembering ordinary difficult times and stretching them into something more than they were. This feels particular enough that I’m not immediately thinking, “Oh yes, this happened before.” But I also don’t keep a perfect record of my moods, especially going back over many years. So… I suppose my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I can’t clearly identify another episode like this.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a hard one for me to judge. Part of me thinks, well, he’s my brother, and what happened shook me, so of course I’d feel it deeply. I don’t want to make it sound as if there’s some “correct” amount of sadness I’m supposed to have, because family things don’t really work that way.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m not seeing it clearly from inside it. Some days I think, maybe this is just grief or strain doing what it does, and other days I think, why am I still feeling so weighed down by it in the way I am? I’m not sure whether that means it’s stronger than expected, or whether I just expected myself to cope differently.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t give you a clean yes or no. It feels connected to what happened with him, definitely, but whether it’s out of proportion, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your life, was there ever a time before these last eight months when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really feel.\n\nThere were definitely times in my life when I was unhappy, or disappointed, or going through something stressful, and I may have felt down for stretches. But when you say depressed or losing interest nearly every day for at least two weeks, I’m not able to clearly point to a specific earlier period and say, “Yes, that was the same kind of thing.” It all gets a bit blurry looking back, especially because I probably didn’t think about it in those terms at the time.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say yes. If it happened, it isn’t something I can clearly remember or identify the way I can describe what’s been going on these past months.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not every single night in exactly the same way, but it’s been enough that I notice it. I’ll go to bed tired, and sometimes I can fall asleep, but it’s uneven—some nights I lie there for quite a while, and other nights I drift off and then wake up very early, around three or four in the morning.\n\nAnd once I’m awake, my mind starts going. I’ll be thinking about phone calls I need to make, or forms, or whether I handled something the right way, whether I forgot some detail. It’s not even always a clear, useful kind of thinking—it’s more like the same worries circling around when I should be asleep.\n\nBy morning I often feel as if I barely rested, even if technically I was in bed for plenty of hours. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think, “Surely tonight I’ll sleep,” and then the same thing happens again.\n\nDoctor: Before these last eight months, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that, no—not with any certainty. I mean, I’ve had periods in my life where I was sad, or worn down, or not myself for a while, like anyone does by this age. But when you ask whether it was this same kind of depression, nearly every day, for at least two weeks… I can’t really identify a time before these last eight months that clearly fits that.\n\nI keep trying to search my memory, and some things blur together. There were hard times, certainly, but I don’t remember it being like this in the same steady, day-after-day way. So I suppose the most honest answer is that I’m not aware of a previous episode like this. If there was one, it isn’t something I can point to and describe confidently.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I’ve noticed a clear change, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a major appetite change that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always use that exact word in my head. It’s more that I keep coming back to my brother and how he declined, and I go over it again and again, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself.\n\nI’ll think, if I had been a better sister, if I’d been paying closer attention, if I had called more, asked different questions, noticed something sooner… maybe I could have prevented some of what happened, or at least kept it from getting as bad as it did. And I know, part of me knows, that I may be expecting too much of myself looking back on it now, but it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s like I should have known. I should have seen it.\n\nSo yes, I’ve felt… not worthless exactly every minute, but guilty, inadequate, like I failed him in some important way. And that feeling has been very hard to set down.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, have you felt noticeably slowed down in your movements or speech?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve noticed, anyway. I mean, I’ve had days where I move more slowly just because I’m tired or not especially motivated, but I wouldn’t say people have been telling me I’m speaking slowly, or that I feel like I’m physically moving through molasses or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do pause more when I’m trying to answer something, because I’m thinking it through or second-guessing how to say it, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I do. It isn’t only when I’m actively thinking about my brother, though of course that can make it worse or bring it into sharper focus.

But even at ordinary times, when nothing in particular is happening, I notice it. Like I’ll be making tea in the kitchen, or just standing by the window looking out at the garden, and everything is technically the same as it always was — the kettle, the trees, the light outside — but it all feels muted somehow. Almost as if a gray film has settled over everything. Not dramatic every second, not sobbing all the time, but this quiet heaviness sitting inside me.

And I try to reason with it sometimes. I’ll think, “There’s nothing wrong in this exact moment, you’re just having tea, the garden is there, the day is going on.” But it doesn’t really lift just because I tell myself that. It’s more like the sadness is already there in the background most days, and then thoughts about him attach to it, rather than it only existing because I’m thinking about him.
